B routine priorityPublic-health news

HPV prevention, local action drive push for a cervical cancer-free Philippines

News Brief
The article highlights the Philippines' efforts to eliminate cervical cancer through HPV prevention, focusing on local action and implementation. It emphasizes the importance of HPV vaccination in preventing cervical cancer, noting that around 95% of cases are linked to persistent high-risk HPV infection. The WHO's 90-70-90 strategy aims to achieve 90% vaccination coverage by age 15, 70% screening for women aged 35-45, and 90% treatment for those diagnosed. Local governments, such as Mandaue City, are working to extend vaccination to private schools and collect data from private practitioners to address gaps in coverage. Health experts stress the need for sustained local efforts, including integrating cervical cancer elimination into annual plans and securing funding through multiple sources. The article underscores the importance of community engagement, awareness, and collaboration to ensure that prevention measures are accessible and effective across both public and private sectors.
Original source text (English)
CERVICAL cancer is one of the few cancers the world has set out to eliminate as a public health problem. For the Philippines, however, reaching that goal will depend not only on national targets but on whether prevention actually reaches girls and women in their communities. At the center of prevention is the human papillomavirus (HPV), a common virus transmitted through sexual contact. While most HPV infections clear on their own, persistent infection with high-risk HPV types can lead to cervical cancer. Around 95 percent of cervical cancer cases are linked to persistent high-risk HPV infection, making HPV vaccination an important opportunity to prevent the disease before it develops, according to the World Health Organization (WHO). The WHO has set a 90-70-90 strategy toward cervical cancer elimination by 2030: 90 percent of girls fully vaccinated against HPV by age 15, 70 percent of women screened with a high-performance test by ages 35 and 45, and 90 percent of women identified with cervical disease receiving treatment. At the recent Association of Municipal Health Officers of the Philippines symposium on “Leading Disease Prevention at the Local Level: The Role of LGUs in Protecting Communities,” health experts brought the global target closer to home: how can proven prevention measures translate into protection that people can actually access in their barangays, municipalities and cities? Closing the gaps in HPV vaccination Get the latest news delivered to your inbox Sign up for The Manila Times newsletters By signing up with an email address, I acknowledge that I have read and agree to the Terms of Service and Privacy Policy . Pediatric Infectious Disease Specialist Dr. Fejelia Faulan, resource speaker and panelist, emphasized that gaps in HPV vaccination cannot be traced to one factor alone. Families, health care providers, schools and communities can all influence whether eligible children ultimately receive protection. ADVOCATING HPV VACCINATION Dr. Fejelia Faulan discusses the role of HPV vaccination and stronger local implementation in accelerating the Philippines’ progress toward cervical cancer elimination. “The gaps are on many sides,” Faulan said, pointing to challenges ranging from parental and community attitudes to vaccine access and hesitancy. She noted that misinformation and resistance to vaccination can make reaching eligible populations particularly difficult. Another important gap is data. While government vaccination programs capture vaccinations delivered through the public sector, Faulan noted that there is no comprehensive national registry capturing HPV vaccination delivered through private practice. In Western Visayas, members of the Philippine Pediatric Society have started gathering vaccination information from private practitioners, which Faulan said could help provide a more complete picture of coverage. “We started that program because we wanted to share our data with the government; so that we can address and minimize the gap,” Faulan said. “We hope that there will be others, other municipalities who will join, so that it can become national data for private practitioners.” The issue goes beyond measurement. If local governments do not know how many children have already been vaccinated through private clinics and schools, it becomes harder to identify who is still being missed and where resources should be directed. Faulan also underscored the importance of widening vaccination reach. The current government program prioritizes girls aged 9 to 14, but she noted that HPV infection is not exclusively a concern for women. HPV can also cause cancers affecting men, including anal, penile and oropharyngeal cancers. WHO likewise recognizes HPV as a cause of several cancers beyond cervical cancer. Local success stories show that ambitious vaccination targets are achievable. During the symposium, Faulan highlighted areas that have reached the 90 percent HPV vaccination target, including Banna in Ilocos Norte and Sampaloc, Quezon, while Ilocos Norte subsequently achieved the target province-wide. “If Ilocos Norte has done this, and one municipality in Quezon has done this, I think we can do this,” Faulan told local health leaders. Making prevention a sustained local priority For Dr. Debra Maria Catulong, city health officer of Mandaue City and Best-Practice Panelist, closing the gap requires LGUs to shift their thinking from detecting disease to preventing it in the first place. “Why are we waiting for people to get sick and diagnose or screen? Why can’t we do something before that?” Catulong said, describing Mandaue City's move toward prevention under the primary healthcare approach. CERVICAL CANCER PREVENTION Dr. Debra Maria Catulong shares Mandaue City’s approach to strengthening cervical cancer prevention through local planning, sustainable financing and expanded HPV vaccination.PHOTO FROM The city is putting this into practice through a cervical cancer prevention initiative designed to complement the national program. While government-supplied HPV vaccines reach eligible girls through the public sector, Mandaue City has allocated local resources to extend vaccination to girls aged 9 to 14 in private schools, helping address a population that may otherwise be missed. The city is also working to establish a mechanism for capturing private-sector vaccination data to better understand its actual coverage. For Catulong, however, vaccination campaigns should not become one-time activities. Sustainable prevention requires LGUs to formally incorporate cervical cancer elimination into their annual plans and identify resources that can support implementation over time. Mandaue City, for instance, is augmenting its health budget through several sources, including its general fund, Special Health Fund and Gender and Development Fund. “Marami kayong pwedeng ma-source out na funds. Ang importante lang, the willingness, awareness and acknowledgement na gusto mong ipasok ’yan na budget should be coming from us,” Catulong told municipal health officers. She added that local strategies should combine vaccination with health promotion and navigation for patients already diagnosed, while tapping private companies, civil society organizations and other partners within the community. “It should be something that’s sustainable. As much as possible, embed it in the plans annually,” Catulong said. “Hindi kaya ito ng isang sektor lang. We all need to work together if we really want to eliminate cervical cancer.” That approach reflects one of the central lessons emerging from the symposium: national targets become meaningful only when they are translated into local plans, budgets and services. Science has already established a pathway toward cervical cancer elimination. Closing the remaining gap will increasingly depend on knowing who is still being missed, making vaccination accessible across public and private settings, sustaining local financing and building partnerships that can carry prevention beyond individual campaigns. For LGUs, the opportunity is to turn the 90-70-90 targets from national numbers into something more tangible: girls protected from HPV today, and fewer women facing cervical cancer in the years ahead
View five news elements
Time
The article was published on 2026-10-27. The event date is not explicitly stated, but the discussion centers on ongoing efforts and future goals related to HPV prevention and cervical cancer elimination.
Location and population
The focus is on the Philippines, particularly on local governments (LGUs) and communities, including specific areas like Banna in Ilocos Norte and Sampaloc, Quezon. The affected population includes girls and women, especially those aged 9 to 14, and the broader community.
Event
The article discusses the push for cervical cancer elimination in the Philippines through HPV prevention, emphasizing the role of local governments in implementing vaccination programs and addressing gaps in coverage. The event is not an outbreak but a public health initiative and policy discussion.
Scale, impact and risk
The WHO's 90-70-90 strategy aims to achieve 90% HPV vaccination coverage by age 15, 70% cervical cancer screening for women aged 35-45, and 90% treatment for those diagnosed. The article highlights the importance of HPV vaccination in preventing cervical cancer, noting that around 95% of cervical cancer cases are linked to persistent high-risk HPV infection.
Response, status and uncertainty
The response involves local health initiatives, such as Mandaue City's efforts to extend vaccination to private schools and collect private-sector data. The WHO and local health experts are advocating for sustained local action, integration of prevention into annual plans, and collaboration with various sectors to achieve the 90-70-90 targets. The status is ongoing and under active discussion.

HPV驱动的细胞死亡途径为宫颈癌 治疗提供了新方向 - EurekAlert!

HPV-driven cell death pathways offer new directions for cervical cancer treatment - EurekAlert!
新闻简报2026 年 9 月 30 日在 Oncoscience 上发表的一篇新综述,题为“宫颈癌 中的程序性细胞死亡途径:串扰、HPV 介导的调节和治疗意义:叙述性综述”探讨了 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV) 如何改变 宫颈癌 中的程序性细胞死亡 (PCD) 途径。该研究强调,HPV,特别是 HPV16 和 HPV18 等高危类型,通过病毒蛋白 E6 和 E7 抑制细胞凋亡,从而导致 治疗 耐药。它还讨论了其他 PCD 机制,例如铁死亡、焦亡、坏死性凋亡和全凋亡,这些机制可能提供替代治疗策略。然而,大多数研究结果都是临床前的,需要进一步的研究来验证其临床相关性。该综述强调了了解这些途径对于改善 治疗 患者的 治疗 结局的重要性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该评论发表于2026年9月30日,活动日期为2026年10月29日,即新闻稿发布之日。
地点与对象
该研究的重点是 宫颈癌,这是一个全球性健康问题,其中 高危型HPV 类型,特别是 HPV16 和 HPV18,是主要原因。
事件
一篇题为“宫颈癌 中的程序性细胞死亡途径:串扰、HPV 介导的调节和治疗意义:叙述性评论”的新综述于 2026 年 9 月 30 日发表在《Oncoscience》第 13 卷上。它讨论了 HPV 如何改变 宫颈癌 中的程序性细胞死亡途径,从而导致 治疗 耐药。
规模、影响与风险
该评论强调,宫颈癌 细胞经常抑制细胞凋亡信号,使它们能够抵抗化疗、放疗和其他 治疗。高危型HPV 通过 E6 和 E7 蛋白直接促成这种抵抗。实验研究表明铁死亡和细胞凋亡之间存在相互作用,但这些发现仍处于临床前阶段。该综述还讨论了其他细胞死亡机制,如细胞焦亡、坏死性凋亡和全凋亡,这些机制对癌症具有复杂的影响,需要进一步研究。
应对、状态与不确定性
该评论是一份同行评审出版物,提供了对 宫颈癌 中 HPV 驱动的耐药机制的见解。然而,大多数新兴方法仍处于试验阶段,需要临床验证。该研究结果基于临床前研究,尚未适用于临床环境。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

HPV-driven cell death pathways offer new directions for cervical cancer treatment - EurekAlert!

News Brief
A new review published in Oncoscience on September 30, 2026, titled 'Programmed cell death pathways in cervical cancer: Cross-talk, HPV-mediated regulation, and therapeutic implications: A narrative review' explores how human papillomavirus (HPV) alters programmed cell death (PCD) pathways in cervical cancer. The study emphasizes that HPV, particularly high-risk types like HPV16 and HPV18, contributes to treatment resistance by suppressing apoptosis through viral proteins E6 and E7. It also discusses other PCD mechanisms, such as ferroptosis, pyroptosis, necroptosis, and PANoptosis, which may offer alternative therapeutic strategies. However, most findings are preclinical, and further research is needed to validate their clinical relevance. The review highlights the importance of understanding these pathways to improve treatment outcomes for cervical cancer patients.
Original source text (English)
Advanced Search Home News Releases Multimedia Meetings Login Register News Release 29-Oct-2026 HPV-driven cell death pathways offer new directions for cervical cancer treatment “This review proposes that PCD in cervical cancer should be understood not as a series of isolated pathways, but as an interconnected regulatory network that governs both tumor behavior and therapeutic sensitivity” Peer-Reviewed Publication Impact Journals LLC Facebook X LinkedIn WeChat Bluesky Message WhatsApp Email image: Figure 4. Schematic representation illustrating the multifaceted role of programmed cell death pathways in the diagnosis, treatment, and prognosis of cervical cancer. view more Credit: Copyright: © 2026 Kumar. This is an open access article distributed under the terms of the Creative Commons Attribution License (CC BY 4.0), which permits unrestricted use, distribution, and reproduction in any medium, provided the original author and source are credited. “This review proposes that PCD in cervical cancer should be understood not as a series of isolated pathways, but as an interconnected regulatory network that governs both tumor behavior and therapeutic sensitivity.” BUFFALO, NY — October 29, 2026 — A new review was published in Volume 13 of Oncoscience on September 30, 2026, titled “Programmed cell death pathways in cervical cancer: Cross-talk, HPV-mediated regulation, and therapeutic implications: A narrative review.” The review examines how human papillomavirus (HPV) alters programmed cell death pathways in cervical cancer, allowing malignant cells to survive, evade immune responses and develop resistance to treatment. By exploring interactions among different cell death mechanisms, the article highlights potential therapeutic strategies to restore cancer-cell susceptibility to destruction and improve responses to existing treatments. However, most emerging approaches remain experimental and require clinical validation. The review was authored by Naina Kumar, affiliated with the All India Institute of Medical Sciences (AIIMS), Bibinagar, Hyderabad, India. Cervical cancer remains a major global health challenge, with persistent infection by high-risk HPV types, particularly HPV16 and HPV18, representing its principal cause. Although vaccination, screening and advances in treatment have improved prevention and care, patients with advanced or recurrent disease continue to face treatment resistance and limited therapeutic options. Programmed cell death (PCD) is a biological process through which damaged, abnormal or unnecessary cells are eliminated through regulated molecular mechanisms. These pathways help maintain tissue health and prevent uncontrolled growth. Cancer cells, however, can interfere with these processes, allowing them to survive despite genetic damage or exposure to anticancer treatments. The review synthesizes literature published through October 2025, drawing on studies identified through PubMed/MEDLINE, Scopus and Web of Science. It examines how different forms of programmed cell death interact with HPV-related signaling, the tumor microenvironment and emerging therapeutic strategies. A central focus is apoptosis, the best-characterized form of programmed cell death. This process removes damaged cells through coordinated molecular events involving proteins such as BAX, BCL-2 and caspases. However, cervical cancer cells frequently suppress these signals, allowing them to resist chemotherapy, radiation and other treatments. High-risk HPV contributes directly to this resistance through two viral proteins, E6 and E7. E6 promotes degradation of p53, a tumor-suppressor protein that normally helps trigger apoptosis in damaged cells. This reduces the activity of pro-apoptotic molecules such as BAX, PUMA and NOXA. Meanwhile, E7 interferes with the retinoblastoma (RB) pathway, disrupting normal cell-cycle control and promoting uncontrolled proliferation. Together, these mechanisms support cancer-cell survival while influencing other forms of programmed cell death. The review emphasizes that overcoming HPV-associated resistance may require targeting several interconnected pathways rather than restoring apoptosis alone. One promising alternative is ferroptosis, an iron-dependent form of cell death caused by the accumulation of damaging lipid peroxides. Unlike apoptosis, ferroptosis depends strongly on iron metabolism, oxidative stress and antioxidant defenses involving proteins such as GPX4 and SLC7A11. Disrupting these protective systems may make treatment-resistant cancer cells more vulnerable to destruction. Experimental studies have demonstrated interactions between ferroptosis and apoptosis. For example, sanguinarine, a plant-derived compound, has been shown to activate both pathways in cervical cancer cells through increased reactive oxygen species (ROS). Inhibiting either pathway can affect the other, suggesting that coordinated activation may strengthen anticancer effects. However, these findings remain preclinical. The tumor microenvironment also influences ferroptosis. Research discussed in the review shows that tumor-associated macrophages can release extracellular vesicles carrying microRNAs that suppress lipid peroxidation pathways, helping cancer cells resist ferroptotic death. Understanding these interactions could reveal additional ways to overcome treatment resistance. Other important mechanisms include pyroptosis and necroptosis, two inflammatory forms of programmed cell death. Pyroptosis involves inflammasome activation, inflammatory caspases and gasdermin proteins that form pores in cell membranes. Necroptosis involves signaling through RIPK1, RIPK3 and MLKL, ultimately causing membrane disruption. These pathways have complex effects on cancer. Under certain conditions, they eliminate malignant cells and stimulate antitumor immune responses. However, persistent inflammatory signaling may also promote tumor growth or immune suppression. Their therapeutic value therefore depends on the biological context and how selectively they can be activated. The review also discusses PANoptosis, an integrated cell death process involving interactions among pyroptosis, apoptosis and necroptosis. Although investigated in several cancer models, direct evidence in cervical cancer remains limited. Further research is needed to determine whether activating these pathways together could improve treatment outcomes. Autophagy represents another important regulator of cellular survival and death. This process allows cells to recycle damaged proteins and organelles. In cervical cancer, autophagy may protect tumor cells during metabolic stress, but it can also facilitate apoptosis or ferroptosis by influencing iron availability, lipid metabolism and mitochondrial function. These opposing effects make autophagy difficult to target. Drugs such as chloroquine and hydroxychloroquine have been investigated as autophagy modulators in combination treatments, but their clinical effectiveness for this purpose in cervical cancer remains uncertain. The review further examines cuproptosis, a copper-dependent form of regulated cell death associated with mitochondrial protein dysfunction. Preclinical research suggests that manipulating copper-dependent pathways may increase chemotherapy sensitivity. For example, baicalein combined with cisplatin has shown effects involving apoptosis and cuproptosis-related signaling in cervical cancer models. Additional mechanisms include parthanatos, associated with excessive PARP1 activation following DNA damage; lysosome-dependent cell death, involving the release of destructive lysosomal enzymes; and NETosis, which involves neutrophil extracellular traps that can influence inflammation and metastasis. These pathways offer further research opportunities, although therapeutic applications remain less developed. Beyond directly eliminating cancer cells, programmed cell death also influences tumor immunity. HPV-associated tumors may evade immune surveillance by altering antigen presentation, interferon signaling and immune checkpoint pathways, including PD-1/PD-L1. These changes can reduce the ability of immune cells to recognize and destroy malignant cells. An important concept is immunogenic cell death (ICD), in which dying cancer cells release signals that stimulate antitumor immunity. Molecules such as ATP and HMGB1, together with calreticulin exposure on the cell surface, can promote dendritic cell activation and help initiate cytotoxic T-cell responses. This creates opportunities to combine cell death-inducing treatments with immunotherapy. The review discusses strategies involving ferroptosis-inducing agents, chemotherapy, radiotherapy and immune checkpoint inhibitors. While PD-1/PD-L1 inhibitors already have an established role in selected patients with cervical cancer, combining them with experimental cell death modulators requires further investigation. The article also highlights potential molecular biomarkers for treatment selection. Gene-expression signatures associated with ferroptosis, pyroptosis, necroptosis and immunogenic cell death have shown associations with patient survival and immune characteristics. However, most require prospective validation before they can reliably guide clinical decisions. Another proposed direction involves targeting HPV E6 and E7 to restore sensitivity to multiple cell death pathways. Combining these approaches with ferroptosis modulators, PARP-targeted treatments or immunotherapy could potentially improve antitumor responses, although these strategies require direct experimental testing. “Overall, the future of PCD research in cervical cancer lies not in identifying additional death pathways, but in strategically integrating them into precision oncology frameworks.” The review emphasizes that translating these findings into clinical practice will require more sophisticated experimental models and validated biomarkers. Three-dimensional tumor organoids, patient-derived models and systems incorporating immune and stromal cells may help researchers better understand how interconnected cell death pathways function within tumors. Important challenges remain. Many proposed interventions have been tested primarily in cell lines or animal models, while some mechanistic evidence comes from other cancer types. Excessive activation of inflammatory or oxidative pathways may damage healthy tissues, and difficulties involving drug delivery, systemic toxicity and tumor heterogeneity complicate clinical development. As a narrative review, the article does not present new experimental findings or a quantitative meta-analysis. The available evidence varies in quality and clinical relevance, and many proposed treatment combinations have not been adequately evaluated in humans. Well-designed clinical studies will be essential to determine whether targeting these pathways improves treatment response, survival or other meaningful outcomes. Overall, the review highlights programmed cell death as an interconnected network linking HPV-mediated cancer development, immune regulation and treatment resistance. Rather than focusing exclusively on apoptosis, the findings support investigating multiple interacting cell death mechanisms as potential therapeutic targets. Integrating these insights with validated biomarkers and existing treatments may eventually contribute to more individualized and effective cervical cancer care.
